L 2 L^{2} -Density of Wild Initial Data for the Hypodissipative Navier-Stokes Equations
Michele Gorini

Abstract
In this paper we deal with the Cauchy problem for the hypodissipative Navier-Stokes equations in the three-dimensional periodic setting. For all Laplacian exponents � < 1 / 3 \theta<{}^{1}\!/_{3} , we prove non-uniqueness of dissipative L t 2 ​ H x � L^{2}_{t}H^{\theta}_{x} weak solutions for an L 2 L^{2} -dense set of 𝒞 � \mathcal{C}^{\beta} Hölder continuous wild initial data with � < � < 1 3 \theta<\beta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}} . This improves previous results of non-uniqueness for infinitely many wild initial data ( [ 8 (C-DL-DR) , 20 (DR) ] ) and generalizes previous results on density of wild initial data obtained for the Euler equations ( [ 14 (D-Sz) , 13 (D-R-Sz) ] ).



Keywords
: Convex integration, hypodissipative Navier-Stokes equations, wild initial data

1
Introduction

The existence of dissipative solutions for the Euler equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v+\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v)+\nabla p=0\\
\operatorname{div}v=0\end{array}\right.

with regularity lower than
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}}_{x}
has been investigated deeply. After the pioneering works of Scheffer
[
33 (Sche)
]
(on the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
) and Shnirelman
[
34 (Shn)
]
(on the periodic torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
), De Lellis and Székelyhidi, in
[
15 (DL-Sz-09)
]
, introduced the convex integration technique (first used by Nash in
[
28 (N)
]
and Kuiper in
[
26 (K)
]
in the context of isometric embeddings, and formalized in a more general setting by Gromov in
[
23 (G)
]
) in this setting, proving the existence of nontrivial compactly supported
L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
weak solutions of (
1
) in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for any
n
. The subsequent paper
[
16 (DL-Sz-10)
]
provided a proof of the non-uniqueness of weak solutions satisfying the weak energy inequality

\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(x,t)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\leq\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(x,0)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux,

i.e. dissipating the total kinetic energy. We call such solutions
dissipative
or
admissible
.

Both of these papers use a Baire category argument, proving that such solutions constitute the set of continuity points of a Baire-1 map. This implies that such solutions not only exist, but are “typical” in the sense of category.

These results were the first steps in the resolution of the second part of Onsager’s conjecture from
[
29 (O)
]
.

1
name=Onsager’s Conjecture,label=thm:spec:Onsager.

Let
(v.p)
be a weak solution of (
1
) and define the total kinetic energy as

E(t)\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(x,t)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux

If
v\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
for
\beta>\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
, then the energy is a conserved quantity, i.e.
E(t)\equiv E(0)
.

By contrast, for any
\beta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
, there exist
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
weak solutions of (
1
) which do not conserve the energy.

The first part was proved in
[
10 (C-E-T)
]
, and refined for more general spaces in
[
6 (C-C-F-S)
,
22 (F-W)
]
.

In
[
17 (DL-Sz)
]
, De Lellis and Székelyhidi introduced a more constructive approach which allowed to obtain infinitely many dissipative continuous solutions (see also
[
18 (DL-Sz-C0)
]
), and then infinitely many
\mathcal{C}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 10$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 10$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$10$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$10$}}$}}-\epsilon}
-Hölder solutions in
[
19 (DL-Sz-C1/10)
]
.

In
[
3 (B-DL-I-Sz)
]
, Isett was able to improve the Hölder exponent for the existence of non-conservative solutions to
\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 5$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 5$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$5$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$5$}}$}}-\epsilon
and, after the introduction of Mikado flows by Daneri and Székelyhidi in
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
, he completed the proof of Onsager’s conjecture above in
[
25 (I)
]
by showing existence of infinitely many Hölder continuous dissipative solutions in the class
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{x}
, for all
\beta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
. This result was later improved to dissipative solutions in the same class in
[
4 (B-DL-Sz-V)
]
.

In the class of admissible solutions, weak-strong uniqueness holds, as proved in
[
36 (W)
]
:
\mathcal{C}^{1}
solutions are unique, and moreover, if such a solution exists, any
L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
solution with the same initial data which is admissible coincides with the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
solution.

However, cleverly adapting and improving the convex integration technique of the above-mentioned papers, the existence of infinitely many initial data giving rise to admissible solutions in the class
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
for
\beta<{}^{1}\!/_{16}
was proved in
[
12 (D)
]
. In
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
and
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
, the following topologically stronger statement was proved: the set of
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
initial data giving rise to admissible solutions is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
. It was proved in
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
for
\beta<{}^{1}\!/_{5}
, and in
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
for
\beta<{}^{1}\!/_{3}
.

Removing the admissibility condition (
1
) leads to non-uniqueness for any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
initial datum, as proved in
[
21 (D-H)
]
.

For the Navier-Stokes equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v+\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v)+\nabla p=\Deltaup v\\
\operatorname{div}v=0\end{array}\right.,

in
[
27 (L)
]
, Leray proved the existence of global weak solutions satisfying the following energy inequality:

\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(x,t)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux+2\cdot\mskip-4.0mu\int\limits_{0\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|\nabla v(x,s)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus\leq\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(x,0)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\qquad\qquad\text{a.e. }t>0.

Such solutions are called
Leray solutions
or
Leray-Hopf solutions
, and we will call them
admissible solutions of (
1
)
. The strategy employed in
[
27 (L)
]
can easily be adapted to prove the existence of solutions to (
1
) satisfying (
1
), which are therefore called by the same names.

Thanks to the Ladyzhenskaya-Prodi-Serrin regularity theory, weak-strong uniqueness for (
1
) holds for
L^{r}_{t}L^{s}_{x}
solutions, where
\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle r$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle r$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r$}}$}}=1
, as proved in
[
30 (P)
]
for
d=3
and in
[
31 (Se)
]
for the general case.

The uniqueness or non-uniqueness of solutions to (
1
) satisfying (
1
) is still a long-standing open problem. The latest step in this regard is
[
1 (A-B-C)
]
where, introducing a body force in the equation, the authors exhibit two distinct admissible solutions on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Several non-uniqueness results have been obtained for non-admissible solutions, i.e. in the absence of the energy inequality (
1
). In
[
5 (B-V)
]
, the authors prove the existence, for any smooth energy profile
e
, of
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}H^{\beta}_{x}
solutions with kinetic energy
e
, i.e.

\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}{\left|v(x,t)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux=e(t),

for some parameter
\beta
. This implies non-uniqueness for the zero initial datum. Choosing a non-increasing
e
, this also implies the existence of solutions of (
1
) satisfying (
1
).

It is known that such
\beta
cannot be too large, since
\beta={}^{1}\!/_{2}
implies weak-strong uniqueness by
[
11 (C-F)
]
. In arbitrary dimensions,
\beta={}^{1}\!/_{2}
is in fact a threshold for weak-strong uniqueness. Indeed, in Terence Tao’s blog post
[
35 (Tao)
]
, the non-uniqueness of
H^{1}_{t}H^{s}_{x}
for any
s<{}^{1}\!/_{2}
has been proven to hold on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
where
d=d(s)
is sufficiently large.

In the subsequent work
[
2 (B-C-V)
]
, a “gluing” theorem is proved: given any two strong solutions
u_{1},u_{2}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}\dot{H}^{3}_{x}([0,T],\mathbb{T}^{3})
, there exists a weak solution
v\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}(H^{\beta}_{x}\cap W^{1,1+\beta}_{x})
, with a set of singular times
\Sigmaup
having dimension
\dim_{H}\Sigmaup\leq 1-\beta
, which coincides with
u_{1}
on
[0,{}^{T}\!/{\!}_{3}]
and with
u_{2}
on
[{}^{2}\!/{\!}_{3}T,T]
. The parameter
\beta
is not quantified in
[
2 (B-C-V)
]
, but the strategy therein allows it to reach at most a value slightly above
10^{-3}
.

Reducing the regularity in time can lead to better spatial regularity. Indeed, in
[
7 (C-L)
]
, the authors prove an approximation result: given a smooth divergence-free field
v
, we can approximate it in
L^{p}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}\cap L^{1}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{x}
with a solution of (
1
), for any
p<2
. The singular set of these solutions is also of low dimension. The strategy of
[
7 (C-L)
]
can be extended to
L^{p}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}\cap L^{s}_{t}W^{1,q}_{x}
, where once again
p<2
, and we have that
s<2
and
q<q_{max}(s,p)
, where
q_{max}(s,p)\to 1
if
s\to 2
or
p\to 2
.

In this paper we are interested in studying how introducing a fractional dissipative term in (
1
) may affect the uniqueness or ill-posedness of the Cauchy problem. More specifically, we consider the hypodissipative Navier-Stokes equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v+\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v)+\nabla p+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}v=0\\
\operatorname{div}v=0\end{array}\right.,

with exponent
\theta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
and admissibility condition

\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(x,t)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux+\int\limits_{0\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|(-\Deltaup)^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}v(x,s)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(x,0)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux.

The previously cited proof of
[
27 (L)
]
can be adapted to show existence for admissible solutions in this case as well. This result is stated in
(??)
. In this case as well, admissible solutions are also known as Leray solutions.

In
[
8 (C-DL-DR)
]
and
[
20 (DR)
]
it was proved that there exist infinitely many
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
initial data for
\theta<\beta<{}^{1}/_{3}
which generate infinitely many
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{x}
solutions which, by
[
9 (C-D)
]
, are in fact
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{t,x}
. In fact,
[
8 (C-DL-DR)
]
produces such data for
\theta
up to
{}^{1}/_{2}
, with solutions that can only be proved to be admissible (i.e. satisfy (
1
)) for
\theta<{}^{1}/_{5}
.

Inspired by the results in
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
and
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
, which prove the
L^{2}
-density of such initial data for the Euler equations (
1
), respectively for
\beta<{}^{1}/_{5}
and for
\beta<{}^{1}/_{3}
, we investigate the existence of an
L^{2}
-dense class of
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
wild initial data (namely data for which non-uniqueness holds) for admissible solutions to (
1
) in
L^{2}_{t}H^{\theta}_{x}
. The strategy proposed in
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
and
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
provides a quantitative criterion for non-uniqueness based on the existence of approximate solutions called
adapted subsolutions
.

Here, we explore and extend that strategy to the hypodissipative Navier-Stokes equations. The main issue with respect to the Euler setting is to control the dissipative term in the energy. Our main results are the following.

Theorem 1.1
(name=
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
weak solutions with data close to
L^{2}
functions and time of admissibility,label=thm:teor:TimeExist).

Let
\theta<\beta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}},w\!\in\!L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
. Then, for all
\eta>0
, there exist a time
T=T(\eta)>0
, an initial datum
w_{\eta}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
such that
\|w_{\eta}-w\|_{L^{2}}<\eta
, and infinitely many weak solutions
v_{\eta}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T],\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\!)
, with initial datum
v_{\eta}|_{t=0}=w_{\eta}
, which satisfy (
1
) on
[0,T]
, but can be proved to satisfy (
1
) (i.e to be admissible) only on
[0,T(\eta)]
. Moreover

\lim_{\eta\to 0}T(\eta)=0.

The fact that the admissibility condition in
(??)
cannot be guaranteed to hold for
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
solutions on a fixed set of times is due to the necessity of controlling the dissipation term in the energy.

Definition 1.1
(name=Wild initial data,label=thm:defi:WID).

Let
X
be a function space. A divergence-free vector field
w\!\in\!L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
is a
(\theta,X,T)
-wild initial datum
for (
1
) if there exist infinitely many weak solutions
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
of (
1
) such that
v\!\in\!X
,
v(x,0)=w(x)
a.e. in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, and the admissibility condition (
1
) holds on
[0,T]
. The set of such data is denoted by
W_{\theta,X,T}
. If
X=L^{\infty}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{x}
, we will speak of
(\theta,\beta,T)
-wild data, and of the set
W_{\theta,\beta,T}
.

As a consequence of
(??)
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 1.1
(name=Density of wild initial data – Hölder solutions,label=thm:cor:DensWIDUnT).

The set
\bigcup_{T}W_{\theta,\beta,T}
is dense in the set of divergence-free
L^{2}
vector fields, for all
\theta<\beta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
.

Moreover, by taking a solution
v_{\eta}
as given by
(??)
, and continuing it with a Leray solution
\tilde{v}_{\eta}:[T(\eta),\infty]\times\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
with datum
\tilde{v}_{\eta}(T(\eta)\!)=v_{\eta}(T(\eta)\!)
, as provided by
(??)
, we obtain the following.

Theorem 1.2
(name=Density of wild initial data – Sobolev solutions,label=thm:teor:DensWIDNoHoeld).

W_{\theta,L^{2}_{t}H^{\theta}_{x},T}
is dense in the sef ot divergence-free
L^{2}
vector fields, for all
\theta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}},T>0
.

The general strategy of the paper is to define suitable relaxations of the notion of solution (the so-called “subsolutions” of Section
3
), and approximating one kind of subsolution with another one which is closer to the notion of solution. This is done constructing sequences of subsolutions that converge, in an appropriate sense, to a “stronger” subsolution. We will need two such approximations, and therefore two convex integration schemes: the first one will converge to a subsolution which is a solution at
t=0
, and has the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
norm of the velocity blowing up at a controlled rate at
t=0
. The second approximation will lead to a weak solution.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
establishes some notations and contains a few useful preliminary results. Section
3
introduces three kinds of subsolutions, namely strict, strong, and adapted. Section
4
contains some important definitions and relations used in the following sections. Section
5
states the approximation results that lie at the heart of the proof. Section
6
deduces
(??)
from those approximation results. Section
7
shows how one can approximate strict subsolutions with strong ones. Sections
8
and
9
contain the two substeps of each convex integration step, respectively a gluing step and a perturbation step. Sections
10
and
11
then prove the other approximation results, namely the approximation of strong subsolutions with adapted ones, and that of adapted subsolutions with weak solutions.

2
Preliminaries

Throughout the paper, we will use the following notations:

•

\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}
are the symmetric 3-by-3 matrices; within this set,
\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{+}
are the positive definite ones,
\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{0}
are the traceless ones, and
\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{\geq 0}
are the positive semidefinite ones.

•

If
R\!\in\!\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}
, we decompose it as

R=\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}\operatorname{tr}R\operatorname{Id}+\mathaccent 23{R}=\rho\operatorname{Id}+\mathaccent 23{R},

where
\mathaccent 23{R}\!\in\!\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{0}
is the
traceless part of
R
.

•

For scalar functions
f
, we write
\nabla f\coloneq(\partial_{1}f,\partial_{2}f,\partial_{3}f)\eqcolon\mathfrak{D}f
;

•

However, for vector fields
v
, we define
\mathfrak{D}v
so that
(\mathfrak{D}v)_{ij}=\partial_{j}v_{i}
, whereas
\nabla v=(\mathfrak{D}v)^{T}
; with these choices,
(v\cdot\nabla)v=\mathfrak{D}v\cdot v=v\cdot\nabla v
;

•

In a similar fashion, for tensor fields
S
,
\mathfrak{D}S
is defined by
(\mathfrak{D}S)_{ijk}=\partial_{k}S_{ij},
whereas
\nabla S
is defined by
(\nabla S)_{ijk}=\partial_{i}S_{jk}
;

•

The Hölder norms are defined as follows

\displaystyle{\left\|f\right\|}_{0}\coloneq\sup{\left|f(x)\right|},\qquad[f]_{k}\coloneq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{3}\\
|\beta|=k\end{subarray}}\sup{\left|\partial_{\beta}f\right|},\qquad[f]_{\alpha}\coloneq\sup_{x,y}\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\left|x-y\right|}^{\alpha}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\left|x-y\right|}^{\alpha}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{${\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\left|x-y\right|}^{\alpha}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{${\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\left|x-y\right|}^{\alpha}$}}$}}

\displaystyle{\left\|f\right\|}_{k}\coloneq{\left\|f\right\|}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}[f]_{i},\qquad\qquad{\left\|f\right\|}_{k+\alpha}\coloneq{\left\|f\right\|}_{k}+\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\beta|=k\\
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{3}\end{subarray}}[\partial_{\beta}f]_{\alpha},

for
k\!\in\!\mathbb{N},\alpha\!\in\!(0,1)
.

Concerning the Hölder norms, we recall the following standard inequalities.

Lemma 2.1
(name=Hölder norm inequalities,label=thm:lemma:HNI).

For
0\leq s\leq r
and
f,g:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle[fg]_{r}\leq{}

\displaystyle C(r)([f]_{r}\|g\|_{0}+\|f\|_{0}[g]_{r})

(2.1)

\displaystyle[f]_{s}\leq{}

\displaystyle C(r,s)\|f\|_{0}^{1-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle r$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle r$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$s$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$s$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r$}}$}}}[f]_{r}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle r$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle r$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$s$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$s$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r$}}$}}}.

(2.2)

Moreover, for
f:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to S\!\subseteq\!\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Psiup:S\to\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle[\Psiup\circ f]_{m}\leq{}

\displaystyle K(d,m)([\Psiup]_{1}\|\mathfrak{D}f\|_{m-1}+\|\nabla\Psiup\|_{m-1}\|f\|_{0}^{m-1}\|f\|_{m})

(2.3)

\displaystyle[\Psiup\circ f]_{m}\leq{}

\displaystyle K(d,m)([\Psiup]_{1}\|\mathfrak{D}f\|_{m-1}+\|\nabla\Psiup\|_{m-1}[f]_{1}^{m}).

(2.4)

Finally, for all
s,r\geq 0
:

\displaystyle{\left\|f\ast\phi_{\ell}\right\|}_{r+s}\leq{}

\displaystyle C(r,s)\ell^{-s}\|f\|_{r}

\displaystyle{\left\|f-f\ast\phi_{\ell}\right\|}_{r}\leq{}

\displaystyle C(r,s)\ell^{1}\|f\|_{r+1}

\displaystyle{\left\|f-f\ast\phi_{\ell}\right\|}_{r}\leq{}

\displaystyle C(r,s)\ell^{2}\|f\|_{r+2}

\displaystyle{\left\|(fg)\ast\phi_{\ell}-(f\ast\phi_{\ell})(g\ast\phi_{\ell})\right\|}_{r}\leq{}

\displaystyle C(r,s)\ell^{2-r}\|f\|_{1}\|g\|_{1},

where
\phi
is a standard mollification kernel, i.e.
\phi\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(B_{1};[0,1])
and
\int\phi=1
, and
\phi_{\ell}\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\ell^{3}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\ell^{3}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\ell^{3}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\ell^{3}$}}$}}\phi(\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\cdot$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\ell$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\cdot$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\ell$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\cdot$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\ell$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\cdot$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\ell$}}$}})
.

For functions
f,g:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote their time-slices by
f_{t}(x)\coloneq f(x),g_{t}(x)\coloneq g(x)
. The above lemma can then be applied to the time slices of time-dependent vector fields, e.g. the velocities of subsolutions, with the notation
\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{r}},[f(t,\cdot)]_{\mathcal{C}^{r}}
for the (semi)norms of the slices. By taking supremum norms in time, the above inequalities can be formulated with
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}\mathcal{C}^{r}_{x}
norms.

We now introduce Mikado flows, the basic building blocks of the perturbations, and the important Stationary Phase Lemma. The proofs of the following results can be found in
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
.

Lemma 2.2
(name=Mikado flows,label=thm:lemma:MikFlows).

For any compact subset
\mathcal{N}\!\subset\!\!\subset\!\mathcal{S}_{+}^{3\times 3}
there exists a smooth vector field
W:\mathcal{N}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that, for every
R\!\in\!\mathcal{N}

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\operatorname{div}_{\xi}(W(R,\xi)\otimes W(R,\xi)\!)=0\\
\operatorname{div}_{\xi}W(R,\xi)=0\end{array}\right.,

and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathop{}\mkern-2.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.56596pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.27084pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-2.0mu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}W(R,\xi)\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mu\xi=0\\
\mathop{}\mkern-2.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.56596pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.27084pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-2.0mu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}W(R,\xi)\otimes W(R,\xi)\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mu\xi=R\end{array}\right..

Using Fourier series in
\xi
and the above integral and differential relations, we obtain that

\displaystyle W(R,\xi)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathclap{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\smallsetminus\{0\}}}a_{k}(R)A_{k}e^{ik\cdot\xi}

(2.8)

\displaystyle W(R,\xi)\otimes W(R,\xi)={}

\displaystyle R+\sum_{\mathclap{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\smallsetminus\{0\}}}C_{k}(R)e^{ik\cdot\xi},

(2.9)

where the coefficients
a_{k},C_{k}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, the
A_{k}
satisfy
A_{k}\cdot k=0,|A_{k}|=1
, the
C_{k}
satisfy
C_{k}k=0
, and moreover

\displaystyle\sup_{R\in\mathcal{N}}{\left|\mathfrak{D}_{R}^{N}a_{k}(R)\right|}={}

\displaystyle{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}_{\mathcal{C}^{N}(\mathcal{N})}\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle C(\mathcal{N},N,m)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle|k|^{m}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle C(\mathcal{N},N,m)$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle|k|^{m}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$C(\mathcal{N},N,m)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{m}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$C(\mathcal{N},N,m)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{m}$}}$}}

(2.10)

\displaystyle\sup_{R\in\mathcal{N}}{\left|\mathfrak{D}_{R}^{N}C_{k}(R)\right|}={}

\displaystyle{\left\|C_{k}\right\|}_{\mathcal{C}^{N}(\mathcal{N})}\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle C(\mathcal{N},N,m)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle|k|^{m}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle C(\mathcal{N},N,m)$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle|k|^{m}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$C(\mathcal{N},N,m)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{m}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$C(\mathcal{N},N,m)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{m}$}}$}}.

(2.11)

In Section
7
, we will need the fact that, if we set

U(R,\xi)\coloneq\sum_{k}a_{k}(R)\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle ik\times A_{k}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle|k|^{2}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle ik\times A_{k}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle|k|^{2}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$ik\times A_{k}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{2}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$ik\times A_{k}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{2}$}}$}}e^{ik\cdot\xi},

then we have that
\operatorname{curl}_{\xi}U=W
. Indeed

\displaystyle\operatorname{curl}_{\xi}U(R,\xi)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\ell mn}\epsilon_{\ell mn}\partial_{m}{\left(a_{k}(R)\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle ik\times A_{k}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle|k|^{2}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle ik\times A_{k}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle|k|^{2}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$ik\times A_{k}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{2}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$ik\times A_{k}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{2}$}}$}}e^{ik\cdot\xi}\right)}_{n}e_{\ell}=\sum_{\mathclap{k\ell mnpq}}\epsilon_{\ell mn}\epsilon_{npq}a_{k}(R)ik_{p}(A_{k})_{q}|k|^{-2}\cdot ik_{m}e^{ik\cdot\xi}e_{\ell}={}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathclap{k\ell mpq}}(\delta_{\ell p}\delta_{mq}-\delta_{\ell q}\delta_{mp})a_{k}(R)k_{p}|k|^{-2}(A_{k})_{q}k_{m}e^{ik\cdot\xi}e_{\ell}={}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{k}a_{k}(R)A_{k}e^{ik\cdot\xi}-\sum_{kpq}a_{k}(R)k_{p}k_{q}|k|^{-2}(A_{k})_{q}e^{ik\cdot\xi}e_{p}={}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle W(R,\xi)-\sum_{k}a_{k}(R)\underbrace{(k\cdot A_{k})}_{0}\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle|k|^{2}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle|k|^{2}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{2}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|^{2}$}}$}}e^{ik\cdot\xi}.

We now introduce a certain “anti-divergence operator” which will be used to obtain the new Reynolds stress
R
in the various approximation results.

Definition 2.1
(name=Anti-divergence
\mathcal{R}
,label=thm:defi:AntiDivR).

Define the operator
\diamond
so that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\Deltaup\diamond v=v-\mathop{}\mkern-2.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.56596pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.27084pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-2.0mu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}v\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}\diamond v=0\end{array}\right.,

and then define

\mathcal{R}v\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 4$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 4$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}(\mathfrak{D}\mathcal{P}\diamond v+(\mathfrak{D}\mathcal{P}\diamond v)^{T})+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 4$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 4$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}(\mathfrak{D}\diamond v+(\mathfrak{D}\diamond v)^{T})-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}(\operatorname{div}\diamond v)\operatorname{Id},

\mathcal{P}
being the Leray projection onto divergence-free fields with zero average.

This operator satisfies the following properties.

Lemma 2.3
(name=Divergence and
\mathcal{R}
,label=thm:lemma:DivAndR).

For any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
vector field
v
,
\mathcal{R}v\!\in\!\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{0}
is symmetric and trace-free, and moreover

\operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}v=v-\mathop{}\mkern-2.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.56596pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.27084pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-2.0mu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}v\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux,.

Moreover, we have the following statement, which we will use numerous times throughout the paper.

Lemma 2.4
(name=Stationary Phase Lemma,label=thm:lemma:SPL).

Let
\alpha\!\in\!(0,1),N\geq 1
. Let
a\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}),\Phiup\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
be smooth functions and assume

\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle K$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle K$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$K$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$K$}}$}}\leq{\left|\mathfrak{D}\Phiup\right|}\leq K\qquad\text{on }\mathbb{T}^{3}.

Then

{\left|\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pta(x)e^{ik\cdot\Phiup}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\right|}\leq C(K,N)\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\left\|a\right\|}_{N}+{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}{\left\|\Phiup\right\|}_{N}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\left|k\right|}^{N}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\left\|a\right\|}_{N}+{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}{\left\|\Phiup\right\|}_{N}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\left|k\right|}^{N}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{${\left\|a\right\|}_{N}+{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}{\left\|\Phiup\right\|}_{N}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\left|k\right|}^{N}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{${\left\|a\right\|}_{N}+{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}{\left\|\Phiup\right\|}_{N}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\left|k\right|}^{N}$}}$}},

and for the operator
\mathcal{R}
of (
2.1
) above we have that

{\left\|\mathcal{R}(a(x)e^{ik\cdot\Phiup})\right\|}_{\alpha}\leq C(\alpha,K,N){\left(\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\left|k\right|}^{1-\alpha}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\left|k\right|}^{1-\alpha}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{${\left\|a\right\|}_{0}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\left|k\right|}^{1-\alpha}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{${\left\|a\right\|}_{0}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\left|k\right|}^{1-\alpha}$}}$}}+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\left\|a\right\|}_{N+\alpha}+{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}{\left\|\Phiup\right\|}_{N+\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\left|k\right|}^{N-\alpha}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\left\|a\right\|}_{N+\alpha}+{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}{\left\|\Phiup\right\|}_{N+\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\left|k\right|}^{N-\alpha}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{${\left\|a\right\|}_{N+\alpha}+{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}{\left\|\Phiup\right\|}_{N+\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\left|k\right|}^{N-\alpha}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{${\left\|a\right\|}_{N+\alpha}+{\left\|a\right\|}_{0}{\left\|\Phiup\right\|}_{N+\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\left|k\right|}^{N-\alpha}$}}$}}\right)}.

We now recall some classical estimates regarding fractional laplacians.

Theorem 2.1
(name=Fractional laplacian and Hölder norms,label=thm:teor:FrLHoeldNorms).

Let
\gamma,\epsilon>0
and
\beta\geq 0
such that
2\gamma+\beta+\epsilon\leq 1
, and let
f:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
. If
f\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{0,2\gamma+\beta+\epsilon}
, then
(-\Deltaup)f\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
, moreover there exists a constant
C=C(\epsilon)
such that

{\left\|(-\Deltaup)f\right\|}_{\beta}\leq C(\epsilon)[f]_{2\gamma+\beta+\epsilon}.

Moreover, for every
\gamma\!\in\!(0,1)
,
\epsilon>0
such that
0<\gamma+\epsilon\leq 1
, and
f
as above, then there exists
C=C(\epsilon)>0
such that

\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|(-\Deltaup)^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}f\right|}^{2}(x)\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\leq C(\epsilon)[f]_{\gamma+\epsilon}^{2}\qquad\forall f\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\gamma+\epsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{3}).

For a proof, see
[
20 (DR)
, Theorem B.1 and Corollary B.1]
.

Continuing, we recall some elementary calculations for the reader’s convenience. With the definitions we gave for
\nabla,\mathfrak{D}
, setting
D_{t}^{(v)}\coloneq\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla
, we have that

\displaystyle\nabla e^{ik\cdot\Phiup}={}

\displaystyle i\nabla\Phiup\cdot ke^{ik\cdot\Phiup}=ie^{k\cdot\Phiup}k\cdot\mathfrak{D}\Phiup

(2.19)

\displaystyle D_{t}^{(v)}(\mathfrak{D}\Phiup)={}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}(D_{t}^{(v)}\Phiup)-\mathfrak{D}\Phiup\cdot\mathfrak{D}v.

(2.20)

Observing that
\mathfrak{D}\Phiup\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1}=\operatorname{Id}
and thus
0=D_{t}^{(v)}(\mathfrak{D}\Phiup\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1})=D_{t}^{(v)}(\mathfrak{D}\Phiup)\cdot\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1}+\mathfrak{D}\Phiup\cdot D_{t}^{(v)}(\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1})
, we can see that

D_{t}^{(v)}\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1}=-\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1}D_{t}^{(v)}(\mathfrak{D}\Phiup)\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1}=(\nabla\Phiup^{-1}\nabla v)^{T}-\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{D}D_{t}^{(v)}\Phiup\cdot\mathfrak{D}\Phiup^{-1}.

We continue by recalling some classical estimates on the transport and transport-diffusion equations, which can be found e.g. in
[
4 (B-DL-Sz-V)
, Proposition B.1]
(transport) and
[
20 (DR)
, Proposition 3.3]
(transport-diffusion).

Proposition 2.1
(name=Estimates on the transport equation,label=thm:propo:EstTrasp).

Assume
|t-t_{0}|\|v\|_{1}\leq 1
. Then, any solution
f
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla)f=g\\
f(\cdot,0)=f_{0}\end{array}\right.

satisfies

\displaystyle{\left\|f(t)\right\|}_{0}\leq{}

\displaystyle{\left\|f_{0}\right\|}_{0}+\int\limits_{t_{0}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}{\left\|g(\cdot,\tau)\right\|}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus

\displaystyle{\left\|f(t)\right\|}_{\alpha}\leq{}

\displaystyle e^{\alpha}{\left({\left\|f_{0}\right\|}_{\alpha}+\int\limits_{t_{0}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}{\left\|g(\cdot,s)\right\|}_{\alpha}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus\right)}

for all
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
and, more generally, for any
N\geq 1
and
0\leq\alpha<1

[f(t)]_{N+\alpha}\lesssim[f_{0}]_{N+\alpha}+|t|[v]_{N+\alpha}[f_{0}]_{1}+\int\limits_{t_{0}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}{\big([g(s)]_{N+\alpha}+(t-s)[v]_{N+\alpha}[g(s)]_{1}\big)}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus.

Define
\Phiup(t,\cdot)
to be the inverse of the flux
X
of
v
starting at time
t_{0}
as the identity (i.e.
{}^{d}\hskip-2.0pt/\!_{dt}\,X=v(X,t)
and
X(x,t_{0})=x
). Under the same assumptions as above we have that

\displaystyle{\left\|\nabla\Phiup(t)-\operatorname{Id}\right\|}_{0}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle|t|[v]_{1}

\displaystyle[\Phiup(t)]_{N}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle|t|[v]_{N}\quad\forall N\geq 2.

Proposition 2.2
(name=Estimates on the transport-diffusion equation,label=thm:propo:EstTraspDiff).

Assume
0\leq(t-t_{0})[v]_{1}\leq 1
. Then, any solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}cl@{}}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta})u=f&\text{in }\mathbb{T}^{3}\times(t_{0},T)\\
u(\cdot,t_{0})=u_{0}&\text{in }\mathbb{T}^{3}\end{array}\right.

satisfies

{\left\|u(t)\right\|}_{\alpha}\leq e^{\alpha}{\left({\left\|u_{0}\right\|}_{\alpha}+\int\limits_{t_{0}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}{\left\|f(\cdot,s)\right\|}_{\alpha}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus\right)}

for all
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
and, more generally, for any
N\geq 1
and
0\leq\alpha<1

[u(t)]_{N+\alpha}\lesssim[u_{0}]_{N+\alpha}+(t-t_{0})[v]_{N+\alpha}[u_{0}]_{1}+\int\limits_{t_{0}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}{\big([f(s)]_{N+\alpha}+(t-s)[v]_{N+\alpha}[f(s)]_{1}\big)}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus,

where the implicit constants depends only on
N,\alpha
.

To conclude this section, we recall classical Schauder estimates (see e.g. the book
[
24 (G-T)
]
), which will be used in several places in this paper.

Lemma 2.5
(name=Schauder estimates,label=thm:lemma:Schauder).

For any
\alpha\!\in\!(0,1)
and any
m\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C(\alpha,m)
with the following properties. If
\phi,\psi:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
are the unique solutions of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\Deltaup\phi=f\\
\mathop{}\mkern-2.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.56596pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.27084pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-2.0mu\int\phi=0\end{array}\right.\qquad\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\Deltaup\psi=\operatorname{div}F\\
\mathop{}\mkern-2.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.56596pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.27084pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-2.0mu\int\psi=0\end{array}\right.,

then

{\left\|\phi\right\|}_{m+2+\alpha}\leq C(m,\alpha){\left\|f\right\|}_{m+\alpha}\qquad\qquad{\left\|\psi\right\|}_{m+1+\alpha}\leq C(m,\alpha){\left\|F\right\|}_{m+\alpha}.

3
Approximate solutions

For the proof of
(??)
, we begin by introducing the various notions of subsolutions needed to perform the convex integration schemes.

The first notion of subsolution is very similar to the one used in
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
,
14 (D-Sz)
,
17 (DL-Sz)
]
.

Definition 3.1
(name=Subsolutions and strict subsolutions,label=thm:defi:Strict).

A
subsolution
is a triple
(v,p,R):\mathbb{T}^{3}\times(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{\geq 0}
such that
v\!\in\!L^{2}_{loc}
,
R\!\in\!L^{1}_{loc}
,
p
is a distribution, the equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v+\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v)+\nabla p+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}v=-\operatorname{div}R\\
\operatorname{div}v=0\end{array}\right.

hold in the sense of distributions in
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times(0,T)
, and moreover
R\geq 0
a.e., i.e. it is positive semidefinite a.e.. If
R\!\in\!\mathcal{S}^{3\times 3}_{+}
a.e., then the subsolution is said to be
strict
.

The next notion of subsolution extends the ones of
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
and
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
. As in
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
, the Reynolds stress is controlled by a power of the trace. However, the exponent
\gamma
will only act on the “reduced” trace
\rho{}^{-1}
, where
\Omegaup>0
is a constant whose role is explained in Section
4
.

Definition 3.2
(name=Strong subsolutions,label=thm:defi:Strong).

A
strong subsolution with parameters
\gamma,\Omegaup>0
is a subsolution
(v,p,R)
such that in addition
\operatorname{tr}R
is a function of
t
only and, if

\rho(t)\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}(\operatorname{tr}R)(t)\qquad\qquad\varrho(t)\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rho(t)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\Omegaup$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\rho(t)$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\Omegaup$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\rho(t)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Omegaup$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\rho(t)$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Omegaup$}}$}},

then

{\left|\mathaccent 23{R}(x,t)\right|}\leq\Omegaup\varrho^{1+\gamma}(t)\qquad\forall(x,t).

Remark 3.1
(name=On strength and parameters,label=thm:oss:StrengthParams).

In our schemes
\varrho
will be sufficiently small so that in particular
\varrho\leq r_{0}
, where
r_{0}
is the geometric constant in
[
14 (D-Sz)
, Definition 3.2]
, thus leading to the conclusion that (
3.2
) implies that our strong subsolutions are also strong in the sense of
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
, provided
\Omegaup=O(1)
(specifically
\Omegaup\varrho\leq r_{0}
). Note also that, if
(v,p,R)
is a strong subsolution for some parameters
\gamma,\Omegaup>0
with
\varrho<1
, then it is also a strong subsolution for any
0<{}^{\prime}<\gamma
with the same
\Omegaup
.

The last notion of subsolution has vanishing Reynolds stress at time
t=0
and the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-norms blow up at certain rates as the Reynolds stress goes to zero. Such adapted subsolutions have been introduced in
[
14 (D-Sz)
,
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
. The blow-up rate in this paper is analogous to the one of
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
. Differently from
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
, the blow-up is controlled by the “reduced” trace
\varrho
rather than the “full” trace
\rho
, and the estimates include a power of
\Omegaup
.

Definition 3.3
(name=Adapted subsolutions,label=thm:defi:Adapted).

Given
\gamma,\Omegaup>0,0<\beta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
, and
\nu
satisfying

\nu>\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1-3\beta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2\beta$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1-3\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2\beta$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1-3\beta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2\beta$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1-3\beta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2\beta$}}$}},

we call a triple
(v,p,R)
a
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
-adapted subsolution on
[0,T]
with parameters
\gamma,\Omegaup,\nu
if
(v,p,R)\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times{\left(0,T\right]})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T}^{3}\times[0,T])
is a strong subsolution with parameters
\gamma,\Omegaup
with initial datum

v(\cdot,0)\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\quad\text{and}\quad R(\cdot,0)\equiv 0,

and, setting
\rho(t)\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}\operatorname{tr}R(x,t)
and
\varrho\coloneq\rho{}^{-1}
, for all
t>0
we have that
\rho(t)>0
and there exist
\alpha\!\in\!(0,1)
and
C\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle{\left\|v\right\|}_{1+\alpha}\leq{}

\displaystyle C{}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\varrho^{-(1+\nu)}

(3.5)

\displaystyle{\left|\partial_{t}\varrho\right|}\leq{}

\displaystyle C{}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\varrho^{-\nu}.

(3.6)

4
Strategy of the proof

The remainder of this paper closely follows the convex integration strategy adopted by
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
in the Euler setting.

Section
5
states results that allow us to approximate one kind of subsolution (as defined in the previous section) with another. One of those results uses the parameters we will introduce in this section in (
4
).

Section
6
proves the main theorem starting from the results of Section
5
.

Section
7
shows how to obtain a strong subsolution from a strict one. By iterating Sections
8
-
9
, we produce sequences
(v_{q},p_{q},R_{q})
of strong subsolutions which converge to a
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
-adapted subsolution in Section
10
, and to a weak solution in Section
11
.

In passing from one subsolution to another, the
\mathcal{C}^{0}
and
\mathcal{C}^{1}
norms of the various subsolutions are estimated in terms of parameters
(\delta_{q},\lambda_{q})
, where
\delta_{q}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
is the amplitude (in space) of
w_{q}\coloneq v_{q}-v_{q-1}
, and
\lambda_{q}
is the oscillation frequency (in space) of
w_{q}
. The parameters, however, are partially different from those chosen in
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
and closer to the ones used in
[
14 (D-Sz)
]
. More precisely, we define

\lambda_{q}\coloneq 2\pi\lceil a^{b^{q}}\rceil\qquad\delta_{q}\coloneq\Lambdaup\zeta_{q}=\delta\lambda_{1}^{2\beta}\lambda_{q}^{-2\beta}\qquad\zeta_{q}\coloneq\lambda_{q}^{-2\beta}\qquad\Lambdaup\coloneq\delta\lambda_{1}^{2\beta},

where

•

\lceil x\rceil
denotes the ceiling of
x
, i.e. the smallest integer
n\geq x
;

•

\beta\!\in\!(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})
and
b\!\in\!(1,\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})
control the Hölder exponent of the scheme and are required to satisfy

1<b<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1-\beta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2\beta$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1-\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2\beta$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1-\beta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2\beta$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1-\beta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2\beta$}}$}}.

•

a\gg 1
is sufficiently large to absorb various
q
-independent constants in the course of the proofs.

The parameter
\Lambdaup
, and thus the distinction between
\delta_{q}
and
\zeta_{q}
, were absent in
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
. They are added here to make sure
\delta_{1}=\delta
, thus making (
7.12
) an
a
-independent estimate. Thus, in particular, we are allowed to bound
\Lambdaup
from below, since such a bound will be satisfied for
a
large enough, but not to bound it from above, which would cause
\delta
to depend on
a
.

With this choice of parameters, we must require the conditions

\displaystyle\Lambdaup\geq{}

\displaystyle 1

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}>\beta>{}

\displaystyle\theta+\epsilon^{\prime},

(4.4)

for some positive
\epsilon^{\prime}
. Condition (
4.3
) merely requires
a
to be sufficiently large.

The main convex integration step will consist in stating that, for a certain universal constant
M>1
, some sufficiently small
\alpha,\gamma>0
, and a sufficiently large
a\gg 1
, if
(v_{q},p_{q},R_{q})
is a strong subsolution satisfying

\displaystyle{\left\|\mathaccent 23{R}_{q}\right\|}_{0}\leq{}

\displaystyle\Lambdaup\varrho_{q}^{1+\gamma}

(4.5)

\displaystyle{\left\|v_{q}\right\|}_{1+\alpha}\leq{}

\displaystyle M\delta_{q}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\lambda_{q}^{1+\alpha}

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 4$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 4$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}\delta_{q+2}\leq{}

\displaystyle\rho_{q}\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 7$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 7$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$7$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$7$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\delta_{q+1}

(4.7)

\displaystyle{\left|\partial_{t}\rho_{q}\right|}\leq{}

\displaystyle\rho_{q}\delta_{q}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\lambda_{q}

(4.8)

\displaystyle{\left\|v_{q}\right\|}_{\theta+\epsilon}\leq{}

\displaystyle M{\left(1+\sum_{i=0}^{q}\lambda_{i}^{\theta+\epsilon-\beta}\right)},

(4.9)

where
\rho_{q}\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}\operatorname{tr}R_{q}
, and
\varrho_{q}\coloneq\Lambdaup^{-1}\rho_{q}
, then there exists a strong subsolution
(v_{q+1},p_{q+1},R_{q+1})
satisfying the conditions (
4.5
)-(
4.9
) with
q
replaced by
q+1
as well as the following additional estimate

{\left\|v_{q+1}-v_{q}\right\|}_{0}+\lambda_{q+1}{\left\|v_{q+1}-v_{q}\right\|}_{H^{-1}}+\lambda_{q+1}^{-1-\alpha}{\left\|v_{q+1}-v_{q}\right\|}_{1+\alpha}\leq M\delta_{q+1}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}.

The proof consists of three steps:

1.

A mollification step, moving from
(v_{q},p_{q},R_{q})
to
(v_{\ell_{q,i}},p_{\ell_{q,i}},R_{\ell_{q,i}})
, where the mollification parameter
\ell_{q,i}
varies on suitably chosen subintervals, as required by the different orders of the upper and lower bounds on
\rho_{q}
in (
4.7
);

2.

A gluing step, which goes from
(v_{\ell_{q,i}},p_{\ell_{q,i}},R_{\ell_{q,i}})
to
(\overline{v}_{q},\overline{p}_{q},\overline{R}_{q})
;

3.

A perturbation step going from
(\overline{v}_{q},\overline{p}_{q},\overline{R}_{q})
to
(v_{q+1},p_{q+1},R_{q+1})
.

The change in condition (
4.5
) with respect to
[
13 (D-R-Sz)
]
was made in order to prevent the new definition of
\delta_{q}
from causing bounds of the form
\Lambdaup^{A}\leq 1
, with
A>0
, to appear in the proofs. Condition (
4.9
) was added in order to control the new trace terms.

Section
8
proves the mollification and gluing steps, and Section
9
addresses the perturbation step. The gluing step was introduced in
[
25 (I)
]
to ensure
\mathaccent 23{\overline{R}}_{q}
is supported in pairwise disjoint time intervals. This allows us to construct the perturbation as
w=\sum_{i}(w_{o,i}+w_{c,i})
, where the
w_{o,i}
are Mikado flows with pairwise disjoint supports and
\operatorname{supp}w_{c,i}\!\subseteq\!\operatorname{supp}w_{o,i}
, thus preventing
w\otimes w
from containing “mixed terms”
w_{o,i}\otimes w_{o,j}
with
i\neq j
, which are harder to deal with.

Fixing
\alpha>0,\gamma>0
, we also define

\displaystyle\ell_{q}\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\zeta_{q+2}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$\displaystyle 1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\zeta_{q}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}}\lambda_{q}\lambda_{q+1}^{2\alpha}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\zeta_{q+2}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\zeta_{q}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\lambda_{q}\lambda_{q+1}^{2\alpha}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\zeta_{q+2}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$\displaystyle 1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\zeta_{q}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}}\lambda_{q}\lambda_{q+1}^{2\alpha}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\zeta_{q+2}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$\displaystyle 1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$1+\gamma$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$2$}}$}}}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\zeta_{q}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-3.5pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle
